========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I’m just constantly getting startled, like way more than normal. At work, if a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool on the floor, I jump like something terrible just happened. Even if someone just walks up behind me and says my name, I’ll about come out of my skin.

And it’s not just the jump, you know? Everybody else kind of laughs it off or goes back to what they were doing, but my heart keeps pounding. I can feel it in my chest and it takes a long time to settle down. I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, like it didn’t bother me, but inside I’m still all keyed up. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I probably needed to talk to somebody, because I can’t keep doing that every day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s both, I guess. The sharp noises are the worst — like if a breaker snaps or there’s that loud pop, or a tool hits the floor out of nowhere, I’ll jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened.

But it doesn’t have to be some huge bang, either. If somebody comes up behind me and says my name, or I don’t realize they’re there, I about jump out of my skin. And the thing is, it’s not over right away. Everyone else moves on, but my heart keeps hammering, and I’m standing there trying to act normal while my chest is still going and I feel all wound up. Even when I know I’m at work and it’s just regular stuff, my body reacts like it’s something dangerous.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wasn’t always like this. I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.

It’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.

I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.\n\nIt’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.

Like when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.

So I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.

And guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.

So yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.

It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.

And when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.

Everybody else kind of laughs it off or goes back to what they were doing, but my heart keeps pounding. I can feel it in my chest and it takes a long time to settle down. I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, like it didn’t bother me, but inside I’m still all keyed up. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I probably needed to talk to somebody, because I can’t keep doing that every day.\n\nDoctor: Are you startled by specific noises or surprises, or can even expected events, like someone you know walking into the room, startle you?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess. The sharp noises are the worst — like if a breaker snaps or there’s that loud pop, or a tool hits the floor out of nowhere, I’ll jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened.\n\nBut it doesn’t have to be some huge bang, either. If somebody comes up behind me and says my name, or I don’t realize they’re there, I about jump out of my skin. And the thing is, it’s not over right away. Everyone else moves on, but my heart keeps hammering, and I’m standing there trying to act normal while my chest is still going and I feel all wound up. Even when I know I’m at work and it’s just regular stuff, my body reacts like it’s something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily startled or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t always like this. I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.\n\nIt’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.\n\nLike when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.\n\nSo I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve being startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.\n\nAnd guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.\n\nSo yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever relive a frightening or traumatic experience in your mind, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.\n\nAnd when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.

And the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.

I don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.

Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.\n\nLike when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.\n\nSo I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve being startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.\n\nAnd guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.\n\nSo yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever relive a frightening or traumatic experience in your mind, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.\n\nAnd when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. Like it’s happening again, right then, and I have to force myself back to where I am. I know I’m not there, I know that, but in that moment my body doesn’t catch up fast enough.

If somebody comes up behind me and says my name, or I don’t realize they’re there, I about jump out of my skin. And the thing is, it’s not over right away. Everyone else moves on, but my heart keeps hammering, and I’m standing there trying to act normal while my chest is still going and I feel all wound up. Even when I know I’m at work and it’s just regular stuff, my body reacts like it’s something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily startled or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t always like this. I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.\n\nIt’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.\n\nLike when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.\n\nSo I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve being startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.\n\nAnd guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.\n\nSo yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever relive a frightening or traumatic experience in your mind, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.\n\nAnd when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. It’s not like my brain plays it like a video where I can start at the beginning and explain it clean. It’s more like I’m just suddenly there again in the worst part of it.\n\nI’ll be doing something completely regular, like loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times, and then out of nowhere I see that flash in my head. And I hear him yelling. The trainee. It’s so clear sometimes it feels like he’s right next to me again. Not like I’m thinking about it on purpose, not like I’m trying to remember. It just hits me.\n\nAnd when it does, it knocks me sideways. I’ll still be standing there with my hands on the tools or whatever, but inside it’s like everything drops out from under me for a second. Like it’s happening again, right then, and I have to force myself back to where I am. I know I’m not there, I know that, but in that moment my body doesn’t catch up fast enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s hard to explain it cleanly. I know you’re asking what happened to him, but the part that comes back isn’t like a full story with a beginning and end. It’s that moment — him yelling. That’s the piece that gets stuck in me.

I’ll be doing something regular, like loading tools in the truck, and then out of nowhere I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear his voice, the trainee yelling, like he’s right there again. It’s not quiet or distant like a normal memory. It just cuts in, and for a second I’m not fully where I’m standing anymore.

And it knocks me sideways. I can still be holding a tool or standing by the truck, but inside it feels like I got thrown right back into it, like it’s happening all over again. I know that probably doesn’t answer it the way you mean, but that’s what I keep remembering — the flash, and him yelling.

Everybody else kind of laughs it off or goes back to what they were doing, but my heart keeps pounding. I can feel it in my chest and it takes a long time to settle down. I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, like it didn’t bother me, but inside I’m still all keyed up. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I probably needed to talk to somebody, because I can’t keep doing that every day.\n\nDoctor: Are you startled by specific noises or surprises, or can even expected events, like someone you know walking into the room, startle you?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess. The sharp noises are the worst — like if a breaker snaps or there’s that loud pop, or a tool hits the floor out of nowhere, I’ll jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened.\n\nBut it doesn’t have to be some huge bang, either. If somebody comes up behind me and says my name, or I don’t realize they’re there, I about jump out of my skin. And the thing is, it’s not over right away. Everyone else moves on, but my heart keeps hammering, and I’m standing there trying to act normal while my chest is still going and I feel all wound up. Even when I know I’m at work and it’s just regular stuff, my body reacts like it’s something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily startled or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t always like this. I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.\n\nIt’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.\n\nLike when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.\n\nSo I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve being startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.\n\nAnd guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.\n\nSo yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever relive a frightening or traumatic experience in your mind, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.\n\nAnd when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. It’s not like my brain plays it like a video where I can start at the beginning and explain it clean. It’s more like I’m just suddenly there again in the worst part of it.\n\nI’ll be doing something completely regular, like loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times, and then out of nowhere I see that flash in my head. And I hear him yelling. The trainee. It’s so clear sometimes it feels like he’s right next to me again. Not like I’m thinking about it on purpose, not like I’m trying to remember. It just hits me.\n\nAnd when it does, it knocks me sideways. I’ll still be standing there with my hands on the tools or whatever, but inside it’s like everything drops out from under me for a second. Like it’s happening again, right then, and I have to force myself back to where I am. I know I’m not there, I know that, but in that moment my body doesn’t catch up fast enough.\n\nDoctor: What happened to the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to explain it cleanly. I know you’re asking what happened to him, but the part that comes back isn’t like a full story with a beginning and end. It’s that moment — him yelling. That’s the piece that gets stuck in me.\n\nI’ll be doing something regular, like loading tools in the truck, and then out of nowhere I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear his voice, the trainee yelling, like he’s right there again. It’s not quiet or distant like a normal memory. It just cuts in, and for a second I’m not fully where I’m standing anymore.\n\nAnd it knocks me sideways. I can still be holding a tool or standing by the truck, but inside it feels like I got thrown right back into it, like it’s happening all over again. I know that probably doesn’t answer it the way you mean, but that’s what I keep remembering — the flash, and him yelling.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid something bad happen to the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / installation_maintenance_or_repair_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"Out of nowhere I’ll see that flash in my head or hear the trainee yelling, even if I’m just loading tools in the truck, and it knocks me sideways like it’s happening again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I know you’re asking if something bad happened to him, and I don’t mean to dodge it. It’s just when I try to answer that straight, my head goes right back to the piece I keep getting hit with.

What comes back is that flash, and his yelling. The trainee yelling. Out of nowhere, like I’ll be doing something completely normal — loading tools in the truck, moving stuff around, not even thinking about it — and then it’s just there in my head. Or I hear him like he’s right next to me. It knocks me sideways, like for a second it’s happening again instead of being something that’s over.

So… yeah, it feels bad. It feels real bad when it comes back. But the part I keep remembering isn’t a clean explanation, it’s that moment and that sound. That’s what I can’t seem to get away from.

breakers, job sites, or even talk about that day, because once it starts in my head I can’t shut it off.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important light, but the exact few seconds of what I did and what I said before he got hurt are just blank or mixed up.\n- key: S074\n  name: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\n  description: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).\n  manifestation: I walk around mad and guilty all the time, like I failed that kid and everybody can see it on me.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: Even when my wife or the kids try to joke with me or something good happens at work, it’s like there’s a wall inside me and I can’t feel happy the way I know I should.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n  description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: I’ve been driving too fast between calls and cutting corners on jobs I know better than to cut, almost like I don’t care what happens to me anymore.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: What brought me in is I can’t stop jumping out of my skin when a breaker snaps, a tool drops, or somebody comes up behind me, and my heart keeps hammering long after everyone else has moved on.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve been written up at work, pulled out of the running for supervisor, and my wife says I’m barely present at home because everything in my life has started revolving around avoiding another blowup like that day.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the arc-flash accident on that retrofit job, about a year and a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / installation_maintenance_or_repair_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S080\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. It’s not like my brain plays it like a video where I can start at the beginning and explain it clean. It’s more like I’m just suddenly there again in the worst part of it.\n\nI’ll be doing something completely regular, like loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times, and then out of nowhere I see that flash in my head. I can still be holding a tool or standing by the truck, but inside it feels like I got thrown right back into it, like it’s happening all over again. Out of nowhere, like I’ll be doing something completely normal — loading tools in the truck, moving stuff around, not even thinking about it — and then it’s just there in my head. I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I know that sounds bad, like I’m avoiding it, but I honestly don’t have that part clear in my head right now.

What I keep getting is him yelling. That’s the part that comes back so hard it makes everything else kind of break apart. When you ask it that direct — did he die — my mind kind of blanks and then jumps right back to that sound, that flash. I can’t give you a clean yes or no from what’s in my head. I wish I could, because I know that’s what you’re asking, but that’s not the way it sits in me.

I know there was an incident, and I know he was there, and I know the yelling is the thing I keep getting thrown back into. But whether I can say that specific thing, like state it plainly… I’m not sure. I don’t want to make something up or say it wrong.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I’m just constantly getting startled, like way more than normal. At work, if a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool on the floor, I jump like something terrible just happened. Even if someone just walks up behind me and says my name, I’ll about come out of my skin.\n\nAnd it’s not just the jump, you know? Everybody else kind of laughs it off or goes back to what they were doing, but my heart keeps pounding. I can feel it in my chest and it takes a long time to settle down. I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, like it didn’t bother me, but inside I’m still all keyed up. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I probably needed to talk to somebody, because I can’t keep doing that every day.\n\nDoctor: Are you startled by specific noises or surprises, or can even expected events, like someone you know walking into the room, startle you?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess. The sharp noises are the worst — like if a breaker snaps or there’s that loud pop, or a tool hits the floor out of nowhere, I’ll jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened.\n\nBut it doesn’t have to be some huge bang, either. If somebody comes up behind me and says my name, or I don’t realize they’re there, I about jump out of my skin. And the thing is, it’s not over right away. Everyone else moves on, but my heart keeps hammering, and I’m standing there trying to act normal while my chest is still going and I feel all wound up. Even when I know I’m at work and it’s just regular stuff, my body reacts like it’s something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily startled or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t always like this. I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.\n\nIt’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.\n\nLike when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.\n\nSo I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve being startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.\n\nAnd guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.\n\nSo yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever relive a frightening or traumatic experience in your mind, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.\n\nAnd when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. It’s not like my brain plays it like a video where I can start at the beginning and explain it clean. It’s more like I’m just suddenly there again in the worst part of it.\n\nI’ll be doing something completely regular, like loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times, and then out of nowhere I see that flash in my head. And I hear him yelling. The trainee. It’s so clear sometimes it feels like he’s right next to me again. Not like I’m thinking about it on purpose, not like I’m trying to remember. It just hits me.\n\nAnd when it does, it knocks me sideways. I’ll still be standing there with my hands on the tools or whatever, but inside it’s like everything drops out from under me for a second. Like it’s happening again, right then, and I have to force myself back to where I am. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. It’s not like my brain plays it like a video where I can start at the beginning and explain it clean. It’s more like I’m just suddenly there again in the worst part of it.\n\nI’ll be doing something completely regular, like loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times, and then out of nowhere I see that flash in my head. And I hear him yelling. The trainee. It’s so clear sometimes it feels like he’s right next to me again. Not like I’m thinking about it on purpose, not like I’m trying to remember. It just hits me.\n\nAnd when it does, it knocks me sideways. I’ll still be standing there with my hands on the tools or whatever, but inside it’s like everything drops out from under me for a second. Like it’s happening again, right then, and I have to force myself back to where I am. I know I’m not there, I know that, but in that moment my body doesn’t catch up fast enough.\n\nDoctor: What happened to the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to explain it cleanly. I don’t… I don’t have that answer clear. I know you’re asking if he survived, and I get why you keep trying to pin that down, but that exact part is where my memory just doesn’t work right.

There are pieces I can remember so sharp it makes me sick — like the smell, and the light, and the way everything looked for a second. Certain bits are clear as day, like they’re burned in. But then the exact few seconds right around what I did, or what I said right before he got hurt… those are just blank, or they get mixed up. I try to reach for them and it’s like grabbing smoke.

I can remember the yelling. I can remember enough to know something went wrong, and that he got hurt. But when you ask me straight, “Did he survive?” my head doesn’t give me a clean answer. It skips around. It gives me pieces, not the whole thing. And I hate that, because it makes me feel like I should know, like any decent person would be able to say it plainly, but I can’t make that part line up.

It just cuts in, and for a second I’m not fully where I’m standing anymore.\n\nAnd it knocks me sideways. I can still be holding a tool or standing by the truck, but inside it feels like I got thrown right back into it, like it’s happening all over again. I know that probably doesn’t answer it the way you mean, but that’s what I keep remembering — the flash, and him yelling.\n\nDoctor: Did something bad happen to the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: I… I know you’re asking if something bad happened to him, and I don’t mean to dodge it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I hear what you’re asking, and I know it’s the important question. But I still can’t say it cleanly like that. When you say, “Did he die as a result of it,” I try to go to that part, and it’s like my brain stops right before the answer.

I can remember pieces so clearly it doesn’t make sense. Like the smell — that comes back hard. And the light, the way it looked in that moment, little details that shouldn’t matter but they’re sharp as day. I can remember enough to know he got hurt, and I can remember the yelling.

But the exact few seconds around what I did, or what I said right before he got hurt… that’s where it goes blank, or it gets all mixed together. I’ll think I have it for a second, and then it changes, or it’s gone. So I don’t want to sit here and tell you yes or no when that part of it isn’t lining up in my head. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just don’t trust what I can pull up right there.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally got me to come in is I’m just constantly getting startled, like way more than normal. At work, if a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool on the floor, I jump like something terrible just happened. Even if someone just walks up behind me and says my name, I’ll about come out of my skin.\n\nAnd it’s not just the jump, you know? Everybody else kind of laughs it off or goes back to what they were doing, but my heart keeps pounding. I can feel it in my chest and it takes a long time to settle down. I’m standing there trying to act like I’m fine, like it didn’t bother me, but inside I’m still all keyed up. It’s gotten bad enough that I figured I probably needed to talk to somebody, because I can’t keep doing that every day.\n\nDoctor: Are you startled by specific noises or surprises, or can even expected events, like someone you know walking into the room, startle you?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess. The sharp noises are the worst — like if a breaker snaps or there’s that loud pop, or a tool hits the floor out of nowhere, I’ll jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened.\n\nBut it doesn’t have to be some huge bang, either. If somebody comes up behind me and says my name, or I don’t realize they’re there, I about jump out of my skin. And the thing is, it’s not over right away. Everyone else moves on, but my heart keeps hammering, and I’m standing there trying to act normal while my chest is still going and I feel all wound up. Even when I know I’m at work and it’s just regular stuff, my body reacts like it’s something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily startled or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t always like this. I mean, I might’ve startled like anybody else if something really loud happened, but not this… not jumping out of my skin over every snap or clang.\n\nIt’s developed over time, and it feels like it’s gotten worse. Now if a breaker snaps or a tool drops, my body reacts before I can even think. And if somebody comes up behind me, even just saying my name, I jump hard. The part that bothers me is how long it stays with me — my heart keeps hammering after everyone else has already gone back to work like nothing happened. I’m still standing there trying to act normal, but inside I’m all wound up.\n\nDoctor: When you're startled and your heart is pounding, are you thinking that something bad is about to happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s like a clear thought, like words in my head saying, “Something bad is about to happen.” It’s more that my body acts like that before I even get a chance to think.\n\nLike when a breaker snaps or pops, or somebody drops a tool, I just… I jump hard, like I’m trying to get away from something. Same thing if somebody comes up behind me and says my name. It’s instant. And then my heart keeps hammering in my chest long after everybody else has already moved on, like they’re back to work and I’m still standing there trying to look normal.\n\nSo I guess, yeah, in the moment it feels like something bad just happened or is about to, but it’s not always a thought I can point to. It’s more like my whole body believes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve being startled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just when something makes me jump. I kind of walk around with this… heaviness, I guess, but it comes out like anger a lot. Like I’m already mad before anything even happens.\n\nAnd guilty. That’s the bigger thing, probably. I feel guilty all the time, like I failed that kid, and I can’t shake it. It’s like I carry it around on my face or something, like everybody can see it when they look at me. Even if nobody says anything, I feel like they know. Like it’s written all over me.\n\nSo yeah, even when it’s quiet, even when nothing’s happening, I’m still on edge in a way. Not always scared exactly… more like wound up, angry, ashamed, and waiting for someone to notice what I already feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever relive a frightening or traumatic experience in your mind, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes. That happens.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It’ll come out of nowhere. I’ll be doing something normal, like loading tools in the truck or grabbing something off a shelf, and then all of a sudden I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear the trainee yelling, like it’s right there again, not like a memory from far away.\n\nAnd when it hits, it knocks me sideways. I can still be standing there holding a toolbox or whatever, but in my head I’m back in it for a second. It feels like it’s happening again, or like some part of me can’t tell the difference fast enough. Then I’m trying to shake it off and act like I didn’t just get pulled out of where I was.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you see or hear in those flashes that come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: It’s usually the same kind of pieces, not like a whole movie from start to finish. It’s flashes. Like I’ll see it in my head real quick, too clear, like that one moment got burned in there. I can be doing something normal, just loading tools in the truck or reaching for something, and then suddenly I’m not really thinking about the tools anymore. I see that flash, and my stomach just drops.\n\nAnd the sound… the yelling is the worst part sometimes. The trainee yelling. It’ll be like I can hear him right next to me, even though I know I’m standing somewhere else. It’s not like remembering a conversation, it’s sharper than that. It cuts through whatever I’m doing, and for a second it feels like I’m back there again, like it’s happening right then.\n\nI don’t always get a lot of detail all at once. Sometimes it’s just that flash, and sometimes it’s the yelling, and sometimes both hit together. But it knocks me sideways. I’ll be holding a toolbox or standing by the truck, and all of a sudden I’m trying to get my face straight because I feel like I just got pulled back into it.\n\nDoctor: What happened with the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that moment with him yelling. That’s what comes back the most. I don’t always get the whole thing in order, like I said. It’s not like my brain plays it like a video where I can start at the beginning and explain it clean. It’s more like I’m just suddenly there again in the worst part of it.\n\nI’ll be doing something completely regular, like loading tools into the truck, something I’ve done a thousand times, and then out of nowhere I see that flash in my head. And I hear him yelling. The trainee. It’s so clear sometimes it feels like he’s right next to me again. Not like I’m thinking about it on purpose, not like I’m trying to remember. It just hits me.\n\nAnd when it does, it knocks me sideways. I’ll still be standing there with my hands on the tools or whatever, but inside it’s like everything drops out from under me for a second. Like it’s happening again, right then, and I have to force myself back to where I am. I know I’m not there, I know that, but in that moment my body doesn’t catch up fast enough.\n\nDoctor: What happened to the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to explain it cleanly. I know you’re asking what happened to him, but the part that comes back isn’t like a full story with a beginning and end. It’s that moment — him yelling. That’s the piece that gets stuck in me.\n\nI’ll be doing something regular, like loading tools in the truck, and then out of nowhere I’ll see that flash in my head. Or I’ll hear his voice, the trainee yelling, like he’s right there again. It’s not quiet or distant like a normal memory. It just cuts in, and for a second I’m not fully where I’m standing anymore.\n\nAnd it knocks me sideways. I can still be holding a tool or standing by the truck, but inside it feels like I got thrown right back into it, like it’s happening all over again. I know that probably doesn’t answer it the way you mean, but that’s what I keep remembering — the flash, and him yelling.\n\nDoctor: Did something bad happen to the trainee that you're remembering in those flashes?\n\nPatient: I… I know you’re asking if something bad happened to him, and I don’t mean to dodge it. It’s just when I try to answer that straight, my head goes right back to the piece I keep getting hit with.\n\nWhat comes back is that flash, and his yelling. The trainee yelling. Out of nowhere, like I’ll be doing something completely normal — loading tools in the truck, moving stuff around, not even thinking about it — and then it’s just there in my head. Or I hear him like he’s right next to me. It knocks me sideways, like for a second it’s happening again instead of being something that’s over.\n\nSo… yeah, it feels bad. It feels real bad when it comes back. But the part I keep remembering isn’t a clean explanation, it’s that moment and that sound. That’s what I can’t seem to get away from.\n\nDoctor: Did the trainee die in the incident that you're remembering?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I know that sounds bad, like I’m avoiding it, but I honestly don’t have that part clear in my head right now.\n\nWhat I keep getting is him yelling. That’s the part that comes back so hard it makes everything else kind of break apart. When you ask it that direct — did he die — my mind kind of blanks and then jumps right back to that sound, that flash. I can’t give you a clean yes or no from what’s in my head. I wish I could, because I know that’s what you’re asking, but that’s not the way it sits in me.\n\nI know there was an incident, and I know he was there, and I know the yelling is the thing I keep getting thrown back into. But whether I can say that specific thing, like state it plainly… I’m not sure. I don’t want to make something up or say it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Did the trainee survive the incident that you're remembering?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t have that answer clear. I know you’re asking if he survived, and I get why you keep trying to pin that down, but that exact part is where my memory just doesn’t work right.\n\nThere are pieces I can remember so sharp it makes me sick — like the smell, and the light, and the way everything looked for a second. Certain bits are clear as day, like they’re burned in. But then the exact few seconds right around what I did, or what I said right before he got hurt… those are just blank, or they get mixed up. I try to reach for them and it’s like grabbing smoke.\n\nI can remember the yelling. I can remember enough to know something went wrong, and that he got hurt. But when you ask me straight, “Did he survive?” my head doesn’t give me a clean answer. It skips around. It gives me pieces, not the whole thing. And I hate that, because it makes me feel like I should know, like any decent person would be able to say it plainly, but I can’t make that part line up.\n\nDoctor: Did the trainee die as a result of the incident you're remembering?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know it’s the important question. But I still can’t say it cleanly like that. I can remember enough to know he got hurt, and I can remember the yelling.\n\nBut the exact few seconds around what I did, or what I said right before he got hurt… that’s where it goes blank, or it gets all mixed together. I’ll think I have it for a second, and then it changes, or it’s gone. So I don’t want to sit here and tell you yes or no when that part of it isn’t lining up in my head. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just don’t trust what I can pull up right there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking it straight, and I know I keep not giving you the answer you want. I’m not trying to dodge it. I just… when you ask, “Did he die?” that’s exactly where my head goes wrong.

I can remember pieces of it clear as day, stupid little pieces that don’t help answer you — the smell, the light, the way everything looked for a second. Those parts are so sharp it feels like I’m back there. But the exact few seconds around what I did, what I said right before he got hurt… that part is blank, or it’s all mixed up. I try to line it up and it won’t stay lined up.

So I can’t honestly say yes or no. I know he got hurt. I know there was yelling. But that specific answer, whether he died, it just isn’t coming through clearly in my memory.

Yeah. I do. I feel guilty all the time, not just when you ask me about it. It’s like I carry it around with me everywhere I go.

I keep thinking I failed him. Like he was this kid I was supposed to be responsible for, supposed to keep safe, supposed to know better than, and somehow I didn’t. Even when I can’t get the whole memory straight, that feeling is there clear as day. It doesn’t need the details to be lined up. It’s just sitting on my chest all the time.

And I’m mad too. Mad at myself, mostly. Sometimes just mad in general, like I’m walking around with my jaw clenched and everybody can see it on me. Like they can look at me and tell, “That’s the guy who let that happen.” I know people probably aren’t thinking that every second, but it feels like they are. It feels written on me.

So yeah… guilty doesn’t even really cover it. It’s guilt and anger mixed together, every day, like I failed that kid and there’s no way to take it back.
